# .NET AI Engineering Academy — Complete Project & Curriculum Handoff

> **Document type:** Developer / AI-assistant handoff + complete curriculum snapshot  
> **Product:** `.NET AI Engineering Academy`  
> **Snapshot date:** 2026-10-16  
> **Source version:** `2.0`  
> **Audience for this file:** A developer, designer, technical writer, QA engineer or AI agent continuing this specific project.  
> **Core audience for the website:** A developer comfortable with C#, ASP.NET Core, Blazor/full-stack web applications, databases and Azure, but a beginner in AI.  
> **Source of truth for current lesson data:** `course-data.json` inside the complete v2 ZIP; `AI_LEARNING_ROADMAP.md` contains the existing human-readable course.

**Read this first.** This is a **handoff of a real existing static website**, not a request to start a different site or a generic AI study plan. This document captures (1) the user's exact educational and UI intent, (2) the inspected state of the supplied v2 website, (3) the entire 360-day curriculum including every day's instructions, (4) 18 project specs, (5) all 51 learning references, (6) all 208 glossary definitions, (7) 12 advanced electives, (8) implementation and progress-state behavior, and (9) what to test and improve next.

## 1. What the user wants — non-negotiable requirements

1. **Beginner all the way to advanced, in order:** A complete foundation-first AI education path for a Microsoft/.NET web developer; no unexplained leaps from 'What is AI?' to RAG or agents.
2. **C#/.NET first:** Start with console apps, ML.NET, ASP.NET Core APIs, Blazor, SQL, Visual Studio, DI, typed DTOs, xUnit and Azure. Python/research frameworks are optional depth, **not** the default implementation language.
3. **Every learning session planned from first to last:** The earlier first-30-days-only experience was rejected. v2 has **Day 001–Day 360** with no gaps. These are **learning sessions, not calendar deadlines**; taking more days per session is allowed.
4. **Hands-on at each step:** Each day should answer: *What is this? Why does it matter in a .NET application? What exact small thing do I build today? How do I prove it worked? What comes next?*
5. **Far simpler teaching than a catalog of buzzwords:** Use plain English, C# analogies, tiny examples, realistic fake business data, screenshots/expected results where beneficial, and progressive disclosure. Define vocabulary before using it.
6. **Project-based sequence:** Every important stage should result in runnable code, tests, and a small portfolio deliverable. Later projects build upon earlier C# services.
7. **Trusted learning references:** Official Microsoft Learn pages, Azure docs and actual C# GitHub samples; supplementary high-quality websites when useful. The user explicitly asked for external website references too.
8. **Preserve the approved design:** The user liked the existing professional, rich-but-simple, responsive **Material Blue** UI. Do not casually redesign it, replace blue with another theme, or introduce a React/Angular/Python requirement for the *learning website*.
9. **No omission of core practical AI engineering topics:** Cover classical ML, LLM basics, prompts and structured outputs, tool calling, embeddings, search, RAG, agents/MCP, multimodal, evaluation, security, Azure/LLMOps, local models/fine-tuning as appropriate. Be candid that no finite curriculum can include every research field of AI.
10. **Usable offline:** The website itself opens locally without an app server, build system, account, API key, paid library, or CDN. External learning resources and some programming exercises naturally need internet or cloud costs.

### User feedback and resulting decisions

- **First roadmap feedback:** Too many disconnected terms and concepts, not clear enough for a .NET developer. Decision: use small numbered projects in dependency order.
- **Website request:** Create a *real HTML website* with Material Design standards, blue theme based on a Material Blue 50–900 palette reference, appealing yet simple, searchable, hands-on. Decision: vanilla static HTML/CSS/JS.
- **First website feedback:** Missing beginner, intermediate and advanced concepts; some parts too difficult; disliked 'first 30 days' emphasis. Decision: v2 expanded to 12 phases, 72 modules, 360 individual lessons, 18 projects, 208 glossary terms and 51 references, with full day-by-day experience.
- **Current request:** Provide a **detailed, complete Markdown handoff**. This document is intended to preserve every aspect of the current project and provide enough context to improve it without relying on chat history.

## 2. Definition of success

A new .NET developer should be able to open the site, finish Day 001 with **no prior AI vocabulary**, work through real C# labs in order, locate the relevant reference repository at the moment needed, get unstuck without a paid course, and eventually build, test, secure, observe and deploy a small enterprise-grade .NET AI application on Azure.

The website succeeds when all of the following hold:

- The first lesson is obvious; next unfinished day is visible; learner progress persists (or warns and offers export/import if local storage is unavailable).
- Every day has an understandable explanation, concrete task, expected evidence, cross-links, and prerequisites already introduced.
- The lesson path is complete, sequential, and consistent with projects, terminology and references.
- The learner can perform early labs with just .NET and local fake data; expensive service requirements are clearly marked beforehand.
- All complex topics have an easier version before the advanced version.
- Enterprise essentials are present: identity/authorization, tenant isolation, prompt-injection protections, human approval for side effects, evaluation, budgets and observability.
- Project samples/commands are reproducible, safe and tested. Starter snippets are clearly distinguished from production-ready runnable samples.
- Material Blue appearance and responsive accessibility remain intact.
- User can download the complete site source, full Markdown course, and the progress backup.

### Start the current website

**Simplest:** Extract the complete ZIP and double-click `index.html` in Microsoft Edge or Chrome. Or open `standalone.html` directly. There is **no learner-side build/install step for the website**.

**Optional local HTTP hosting for development/testing:** Open that extracted directory in VS Code with a static web server/Live Server extension, or use any local static file host. Do not turn the learner website into an ASP.NET Core application unless the user explicitly asks; the *learner's projects* use .NET, but this learning platform is deliberately lightweight and offline.

**Learning project's own environment:** C#/.NET SDK, Visual Studio or VS Code C# support, Git, xUnit. Later lessons add providers, Azure services, vector stores or Agent Framework when needed. Do not install everything on Day 1.

## 4. Current website architecture (source-backed)

```text
Browser / local HTML website (no backend, no required internet)
  |
  |-- index.html (HTML page shell and SVG icons)
  |-- styles.css (Material Blue-style theme / responsiveness)
  |-- course-data.js (const COURSE loaded before app.js)
  `-- app.js
        |
        |-- UI: Overview / Learning path / Projects / Daily lesson / Glossary / Resources
        |-- URL hash navigation (#overview, #path, #projects, #kickstart, ...)
        |-- Filters + text search
        |-- Daily and project checkboxes
        |-- Browser localStorage (`dotnet-ai-academy-progress-v2`)
        |-- JSON progress export/import/reset
        `-- External reference links (open separate tab when clicked)

Standalone edition: standalone.html embeds corresponding CSS, data and app JS.

Separately, LEARNER-BUILT .NET PROJECTS evolve from:
C# / ML.NET -> .NET model API -> Blazor/ASP.NET -> tools -> embeddings
-> vector DB -> PDF RAG -> advanced RAG -> Agent Framework -> MCP
-> multimodal -> Azure enterprise capstone.
```

### There are six primary website screens

1. **Overview:** Hero/Start, course counts, next unfinished day, project entry and progress.
2. **Learning path:** Twelve collapsible phases, 72 modules, beginner/intermediate/advanced filter, module explanations, analogy, code snippet, five task/check items and references.
3. **Build projects:** Eighteen project cards with stack, input/output, implementation task list, acceptance statement and project-level completion toggle.
4. **Day 1–360:** One day at a time: Understand → Tiny task → Check result → .NET analogy → module C# snippet → references → completion → previous/next. Module selector, day search, phase strip and continue button.
5. **AI glossary:** Search and filter 208 short explanations by category.
6. **Setup & resources:** Bootstrap commands, repo structure, official resource catalog, advanced electives, setup checklist, and progress export/import/reset.

### User interactions to preserve

- The global search field searches **modules** and navigates to the Learning path; it is *not* a universal search over every screen. Daily lessons and glossary have their own text searches.
- Day search displays up to 30 matching day entries per query; all 360 remain reachable by day navigation or module selection.
- Phase headers expand/collapse, module details update, sidebar works responsively, and learning path filters by level.
- `Continue` finds the next unfinished daily lesson; when all days are complete, the final day remains available for review.
- Day complete checkboxes affect daily progress and module counters; full module completion requires five marked days.
- Project completed toggles are **per project**, not per project subtask. Individual project subtasks are currently plain list items.
- Setup checkboxes are separate from day/project completion.
- Export downloads a JSON backup; import asks before replacing current state; reset asks for confirmation.
- URLs use hash-based navigation; links to external sites use `target="_blank"` and `rel="noopener noreferrer"`.

## 5. Source-level schemas and data contracts

`course-data.json` contains these top-level fields:

```json
{
  "title": ".NET AI Engineering Academy",
  "version": "2.0",
  "days": [],
  "phases": [],
  "modules": [],
  "projects": [],
  "resources": [],
  "glossary": [],
  "electives": []
}
```

**Stable IDs:** Phases use `phase01`…`phase12`; modules use two-digit string IDs `"01"`…`"72"`; days use integer `1`…`360`; projects use `"p0"`…`"p17"`; resources use short string keys such as `"mlsentiment"`. Maintain referential integrity when editing.

**Day record:** `{ d, title, module, stage, explain, task, output, refs[] }`.

**Module record:** `{ id, phase, title, week, hours, level, project, goal, why, analogy, example, build, concepts[], recipe[5], verify[5], avoid, refs[], days[5], start, end, code:{label,code} }`.

**Project record:** `{ id, n, title, module, level, summary, stack, input, output, tasks[], accept, refs[] }`.

**Phase record:** `{ id, n, title, sub, level, accent, modules[6], milestone, check[], weeks }`.

**Resource record:** `{ id, title, url, kind, group, note }`.

**Glossary item:** `[term, plainEnglishDefinition, category]` (an array triple, not an object).

**Elective record:** `{ title, tag, icon, why, try, refs[] }`.

### Editing and publishing the course safely

1. Edit **`course-data.json`** as the human-maintainable structured content source.
2. Validate JSON, IDs, ranges, references, text coverage and modules/projects progression.
3. Regenerate **`course-data.js`** as `const COURSE = ` + pretty-printed JSON + `;` (no network fetch; the website works under `file://`).
4. Regenerate the **standalone HTML** by inlining the *current* `styles.css`, `course-data.js` and `app.js` into a copy of `index.html`; avoid leaving old embedded data in `standalone.html`.
5. Regenerate / review the **human Markdown course** so it agrees with the JSON; never hand-edit only one of the four content representations and assume the other three updated.
6. Update displayed counts and README if phases, modules, days, projects, references or glossary totals change.
7. Re-test progress compatibility. Do not silently recycle old day numbers for unrelated lessons.
8. Zip the **entire eight-file folder**, verify ZIP contents, and separately verify the standalone file. A broken loose directory is not an acceptable release.

**Recommended next:** Add a scripted build/validation pipeline to prevent JSON/JS/HTML/Markdown drift and automatically assert counts, references and HTML links. That pipeline is not currently in the ZIP.

## 6. Progress format and migration rules

The current code uses browser `localStorage` under the exact key **`dotnet-ai-academy-progress-v2`**.

```json
{
  "days": { "1": true, "2": true },
  "projects": { "p0": true },
  "setup": { "0": true }
}
```

Only literal `true` entries are restored; absent/false entries are unfinished. The sanitized progress object has exactly the `days`, `projects` and `setup` maps.

Exported backup format:

```json
{
  "app": "DotNetAiAcademy",
  "version": 2,
  "exportedAt": "2026-10-16T00:00:00.000Z",
  "progress": {
    "days": { "1": true },
    "projects": {},
    "setup": {}
  }
}
```

- Example export date is illustrative, not a claim of a real user backup.
- File name: `dotnet-ai-academy-v2-progress.json`.
- Import rejects files over 2 MB, wrong `app` or wrong `version`, and requests overwrite confirmation.
- Clearing browser storage erases progress unless backed up. Local file privacy restrictions can block saving; the code shows a toast warning.
- There is **no account-based synchronization**, server database, multi-device sync, or old v1-to-v2 progress mapping.
- Changes to lesson/day IDs may invalidate learners' saved history. If extending the curriculum, implement explicit versioned migrations or preserve existing IDs.

## 7. Material Blue design contract — preserve

**USER REQUIREMENT:** The current UI, general layout and blue palette have been approved. Any further work should make learning easier without a visual reset.

Verified CSS variables:

| Token | Hex |
|---|---|
| Blue 50 | `#E3F2FD` |
| Blue 100 | `#BBDEFB` |
| Blue 200 | `#90CAF9` |
| Blue 300 | `#64B5F6` |
| Blue 400 | `#42A5F5` |
| Blue 500 | `#2196F3` |
| Blue 600 | `#1E88E5` |
| Blue 700 (primary actions) | `#1976D2` |
| Blue 800 | `#1565C0` |
| Blue 900 | `#0D47A1` |

| Cards | `#FFFFFF` |
| Primary text | `#15243A` |
| Default border | `#E4EBF4` |

Core style: generous whitespace, modern white cards, soft-blue secondary surfaces, dark legible text, rounded corners (~18px), restrained shadows, blue navigation selection, blue buttons, progress bars, clean icons. System-friendly font stack `Inter, Roboto, Segoe UI, ...`; no font-download requirement.

Responsive breakpoints present in source include ~1360, 1240, 1160, 980, 840, 760, 600 and 520 px. Desktop uses a fixed navigation sidebar (257px default); around 980px it becomes a toggled mobile drawer. Focus-visible states and prefers-reduced-motion handling are already present.

**Not a library dependency:** 'Material Design–inspired' means the visual style; this is **not** React Material UI or Angular Material. Don't introduce a large UI framework without a concrete advantage and user approval.

### UI improvements allowed without losing the design

- Increase explanatory readability, add clear prerequisites and expandable solved examples.
- Use info/help accordions, breadcrumbs, progress, quizzes and nonintrusive evidence submission fields.
- Keep mobile content readable; no horizontal overflow, clipped buttons or tiny tap targets.
- Improve accessibility: native controls, heading hierarchy, form labels, keyboard focus, contrast, screen reader status and large-target click areas.
- Keep blue primary; status colors used sparingly, not for decoration.

## 8. Learning experience and instructional design contract

The **current day template** is: **Understand it first → Try a tiny task → Check your result** plus module analogy, example C# snippet, official links and completion checkbox. This is a strong base but some learners still find 360 short entries insufficient without worked examples.

**RECOMMENDED NEXT:** Enrich difficult lessons to use the following beginner-centered format, without replacing the existing 360-day sequence:

1. **What you already know:** Example: 'You know how an ASP.NET endpoint maps input to an output.'
2. **One new idea only:** Define unfamiliar terms on first use.
3. **Why it matters:** Explain a real C#/Azure business use-case and a case where AI is *not* appropriate.
4. **Tiny example:** Include sample input and expected output; add a diagram only when it clarifies.
5. **Exact hands-on steps:** Prerequisites, `dotnet new`/`dotnet add package` command if needed, specific file/class to create, short copyable runnable code, unit test or API request.
6. **Expected proof:** A deterministic test, screenshot, sample output, generated file, or evaluation metric; note AI outputs may vary.
7. **Common mistakes and troubleshooting:** Missing model access, key in client code, wrong embedding dimension, empty source, broken import, tenant leak, model variability, etc.
8. **Self-check:** 2–3 questions with short answer explanations, not only a completion checkbox.
9. **Official references:** One required .NET source and one optional extra; do not force users to read 10 repos.
10. **What comes tomorrow / which project it advances:** Clear dependency and continuity.

Prefer a **worked exercise before an independent challenge**, particularly for vectors, probabilities, training, tokenization, tool calling, RAG, agent orchestration, and evaluation. Maintain a 'show more' for difficult optional theory.

### Project quality standard

Each practical project should eventually provide:

- Problem in plain language, why ordinary C# alone is/isn't sufficient, and what the AI component is responsible for.
- Explicit prerequisites/day/module links; low-cost local or fake-data path where possible.
- Solution diagram; suggested ASP.NET/Blazor layers; named project/folder/classes and data models.
- Commands with pinned or supported .NET/runtime/API versions and a clear secret-management strategy.
- Sample dataset / fake records, expected requests and responses, runnable minimal happy path.
- Error cases, validations, authorization and tests; **xUnit** baseline where appropriate.
- Acceptance criteria a beginner can demonstrate, including how to know the work is done.
- References to an official C# repo and an explanation of which folder/file to study first.
- Optional extension: cost, quality evaluation, deployment and a short README/project write-up.

### Stack and teaching order

Early projects: `C# + .NET console + xUnit + ML.NET`, with no API key. Next: official provider .NET SDK, ASP.NET Core, Blazor, C# DTOs and dependency injection. Then tools, `float[]`/LINQ embeddings, pgvector/Qdrant/Azure AI Search, document RAG, agent SDK/MCP. Finally secure Azure deploys with Entra ID, Key Vault, telemetry and CI/CD. `Microsoft.Extensions.AI` is introduced after direct API experience so learners understand its abstractions. Use **Microsoft Agent Framework** for modern agent labs; Semantic Kernel is useful as legacy/reference context, not the primary new-agent teaching path. APIs/product names evolve, so check official docs before prescribing exact package/SDK versions.

## 9. Full curriculum stage summary

The **current v2** has **12 phases x 6 modules x 5 daily sessions = 360 sessions**. There is no 30-day course cutoff. Each phase is 30 learning sessions; project implementations may take additional real-world time.

| Phase | Days | Main outcome |
|---|---|---|
| 01 | 001–030 | Explain AI vs ordinary code, identify ML/GenAI tasks and prepare a safe C# environment |
| 02 | 031–060 | Grasp vectors, cosine similarity, probability, splits, evaluation and responsible data decisions |
| 03 | 061–090 | Train/evaluate ML.NET classification, regression and related predictive patterns, expose an API |
| 04 | 091–120 | Explain neural networks, gradient intuition, attention, transformers, tokens and model generation |
| 05 | 121–150 | Build safe C# model calls, stateful/streamed console and Blazor/ASP.NET chat |
| 06 | 151–180 | Prompt design, context engineering, typed structured output, validation and reliable UI/API |
| 07 | 181–210 | Secure C# function tools, embeddings, semantic search and vector indexes |
| 08 | 211–240 | BM25, hybrid search, document ingestion/chunking, basic RAG, citations and no-answer |
| 09 | 241–270 | Retrieval evaluation, advanced RAG, ACL/tenant filtering, lifecycle and .NET AI abstractions |
| 10 | 271–300 | Bounded agents, state/memory, human approvals, multi-agent workflows and MCP/A2A |
| 11 | 301–330 | Document/vision/audio processing, local LLMs, routing, RAG-vs-fine-tuning, ONNX optimization |
| 12 | 331–360 | LLM security, evaluation, LLMOps, resilience, Azure deployment, CI/CD and final capstone |

## 10. Suggested .NET learner repository and increasing project architecture

The learner's solution code is **separate** from the static academy website source. A reasonable learning repository:

```text
dotnet-ai-learning/
├── 01-ai-fundamentals/
├── 02-vector-and-statistics/
├── 03-mlnet-sentiment/
├── 04-delivery-regression/
├── 05-console-llm-chat/
├── 06-blazor-chat/
├── 07-structured-text-api/
├── 08-order-tool-calling/
├── 09-semantic-product-search/
├── 10-pgvector-search/
├── 11-basic-rag/
├── 12-enterprise-rag/
├── 13-agent-and-workflows/
├── 14-mcp-csharp/
├── 15-multimodal/
├── 16-evaluation-security/
├── 17-azure-capstone/
└── README.md
```

These learner folders are examples, **not a claim** that the site ZIP includes runnable implementations of those 18 exercises. The website primarily holds lesson plans, prompts, snippets and references. If a follow-on developer produces actual starter code, package it separately with its own README and tests, then link to it from the appropriate lesson.

**Recommended enterprise capstone pattern:**

```text
Blazor (authenticated UI)
       |
ASP.NET Core (API + business rules + auth + validation)
       |
Application/AI Services (DI, DTOs, logging)
       |-------- IChatClient / model gateway -> model provider
       |-------- Document service -> Blob Storage -> extractor -> chunks
       |-------- Retrieval -> Azure AI Search -> ranker -> grounded prompt
       |-------- Tool service -> authorized DB/API calls
       |-------- Bounded agent/workflow -> optional MCP clients, approvals
       `-------- Evaluation + telemetry + cost/security controls
Infrastructure: Entra ID, managed identity, Key Vault, Azure hosting,
                SQL/Storage, monitoring, CI/CD, IaC and controlled budgets.
```

**Security rule:** Model responses never become trusted database updates, authorization checks, or arbitrary C#/SQL execution. Authorization always lives in real backend services. The LLM proposes tool calls; C# validates and executes allowed operations only.

## 12. Important gaps, risks, and next-release backlog

The user still finds some AI learning material intimidating. A long list of topics can be comprehensive yet **still not teach well**. Do **not** assume that 360 distinct entries automatically equal mastery. Prioritize **better examples, runnable labs and checks** over increasing the module count merely for marketing.

### Priority 0 — preserve usability and avoid source loss

- [ ] Start from extracted **complete ZIP**; do not start from incomplete loose folder.
- [ ] Include this handoff in any next release and package all CSS/JS/data files.
- [ ] Keep original v2 ZIP unchanged as a recoverable snapshot.
- [ ] Add a repeatable build that regenerates JSON-derived JS/standalone/Markdown consistently.
- [ ] Provide deterministic validator for phase/day/module/project/resource integrity and release ZIP completeness.
- [ ] Smoke-test file-open and local-server behavior; progress/export/import, navigation, mobile drawer and all internal links.

### Priority 1 — make the lessons genuinely easy for a beginner

- [ ] Each of 360 sessions should link to an exact prerequisite, a *worked* example and a sample result—not only a short task description.
- [ ] Add the actual C# console/API starter project(s) for lessons that say 'write/build', or link directly to the relevant C# sample path.
- [ ] Add tiny checkpoints/quizzes with explanations, common errors and hints.
- [ ] Add an optional 'I am stuck' hint or worked-solution reveal for algebra, similarity, ML.NET data pipelines, LLM messages, RAG, tools, and agent workflows.
- [ ] Add cost/prerequisite tags: `Offline`, `Free local`, `API key`, `Azure account`, `Optional hardware`.
- [ ] Show learning dependencies and make the 'why am I learning this?' concrete for .NET apps.
- [ ] Make early definitions stable, plain English, and discoverable directly from lessons.
- [ ] Make lab steps unambiguous: exact file and class names, `dotnet` commands, fake input, expected output, test failure path, definition of done.
- [ ] Link actual project repos/branches/sample folders, not generic GitHub landing pages wherever a specific runnable lab exists.

### Priority 2 — audit/extend practical Microsoft and .NET AI topics

**Foundations and reliable engineering**
- [ ] AI task suitability/build-vs-buy, data pipeline, train/validate/test, leakage, metrics, math intuition; distinguish prediction from certainty.
- [ ] Introduce .NET HTTP integrations (`HttpClientFactory`, typed clients), `CancellationToken`, async streaming/SSE, dependency injection, resilience and structured logging as AI-relevant web skills.
- [ ] Add clear provider/model/deployment/API-version vocabulary, model context limits, rate limits, token cost and supported feature differences.
- [ ] Add prompt versioning, instructions vs data separation, few-shot patterns, JSON/schema validation and deterministic post-processing.
- [ ] Introduce confidence/calibration caveats (model 'confidence' is not guaranteed correctness) and correct train/eval terminology.

**LLMs, embeddings, retrieval and RAG**
- [ ] Depth as appropriate: sampling (temperature, top-p), token budgets, attention, causal masking, KV cache, context compaction, inference, response streaming.
- [ ] More hands-on dimension/normalization/similarity experiments, storing embedding model/version and migrating vectors.
- [ ] Explicit BM25 vs embeddings, hybrid/RRF, filters, approximate nearest neighbors/HNSW, vector storage options and tradeoffs.
- [ ] More hands-on document ingestion: PDFs with page/table provenance, DOCX, OCR, extraction validation, metadata, PII, chunk overlap, changes/deletes/re-indexing.
- [ ] RAG baselines vs advanced: retrieval metrics, grounding, rerankers, query rewrite, context selection, multi-hop, freshness, answer abstention and citation checking.
- [ ] Distinguish enterprise structured SQL lookup from document RAG: use actual tools/query services for authoritative transactional data.

**Agents, protocols, and applications**
- [ ] Teach 'ordinary workflow → tool-calling loop → agent' in that order; never jump directly to a multi-agent framework.
- [ ] Cover tool schemas, strict server-side authorization, replay/idempotency, max iterations, fail closed, human approval and durable workflow state.
- [ ] Add Microsoft Agent Framework examples with actual current .NET APIs; label Semantic Kernel material as historical/migration reference where appropriate.
- [ ] MCP tool/resource/prompt model, C# SDK server+client, available transports, tool discovery, auth boundaries, and where A2A fits.
- [ ] Explicit session state, short-term vs long-term memory, retrieval of user context, minimization, retention and secure deletion.

**Multimodal, model management and production**
- [ ] Real .NET integration labs for Azure Document Intelligence, Vision, Speech; human correction of extracted fields, error cases and privacy.
- [ ] Understand local Ollama/ONNX tradeoffs, CPU/GPU limits, quantization, latency; no compulsory Python for general application engineering.
- [ ] 'Use RAG vs fine-tune vs classical ML vs deterministic code' decision tree with worked business cases; fine-tuning remains optional advanced depth.
- [ ] Evaluation golden datasets, per-stage retrieval/evidence/answer quality, agent trace evaluation, LLM-as-judge caveats, confidence intervals and regression gates.
- [ ] Azure Foundry provider/agent services, Azure AI Search, managed identity/Entra, Key Vault, Blob, SQL, Azure Monitor/App Insights, .NET Aspire where appropriate, infrastructure as code, budgets and deployment rollback.
- [ ] LLMOps: cost/latency/tokens, tracing, model and prompt versions, cache, routing, retries/timeouts, rate limiting, circuit breakers, fallback, audit and incident response.
- [ ] Microsoft responsible AI, OWASP LLM threats, prompt injection, tenant isolation, PII handling, secrets, output rendering safety, data residency and governance.

**Additional worthwhile electives (not necessary before finishing core)**
- [ ] Semantic Kernel migration reference, AI Search knowledge store patterns, GraphRAG, SQL vector support, model routing/gateways, ONNX optimization, classical ML experiment design, A/B testing, agent interoperability, Azure Machine Learning MLOps, deeper neural math and PyTorch reading ability.

### Priority 3 — product and quality experience

- [ ] Consider per-project subtask progress rather than only project-level completion.
- [ ] Allow learner notes and evidence links per lesson (prefer opt-in local export; no service needed).
- [ ] Add a course index/TOC and richer 'related glossary term' deep-links on every lesson.
- [ ] Verify WCAG keyboard and screen reader accessibility, focus return after hash navigation and color contrast.
- [ ] Test mobile widths ~320–420px, tablet, laptop, desktop; preserve blue UI.
- [ ] Create a link checker for official websites; fix redirects and broken GitHub paths without silently replacing C# examples with Python.
- [ ] Add offline static-site checks and explicit 'requires internet to open reference' copy.
- [ ] Consider structured learner assessments and a capstone rubric: correctness, security, evidence, cost, maintainability and presentation.

### What must *not* happen in the next iteration

- Do not shrink the roadmap to just the first 30 days.
- Do not require Python, React, a GPU or paid Azure services on Day 1.
- Do not dump 100 new advanced terms into the beginner stage without prerequisites.
- Do not claim every example is runnable if it is only a conceptual snippet.
- Do not claim the website has a user account, cloud sync, or an embedded AI tutor; it currently does not.
- Do not drop the localStorage backup feature, responsive sidebar, projects, references or glossary.
- Do not replace the established blue Material-style site without explicit user feedback.
- Do not commit API keys or realistic sensitive company/customer information to GitHub or browser JavaScript.
- Do not turn an LLM response into unrestricted shell, SQL or business-side-effect execution.

## 13. QA checklist and release acceptance

**Data checks (automate):**

- [ ] `days.length` equals 360 (or intended new documented total), day numbers unique/gapless.
- [ ] Exactly 5 days per module and 6 modules per phase in v2; module `start`/`end` agrees with `days` IDs.
- [ ] Every daily explanation/task/proof is nonempty and distinct; unexplained jargon is flagged by content review.
- [ ] Every phase, module, day, project and elective references valid resource IDs.
- [ ] Every module's referenced project exists; all resources have valid HTTPS URLs; explicit optional labels are truthful.
- [ ] Canonical JSON matches generated JS and standalone edition; Markdown curriculum regenerated and complete.
- [ ] README and UI statistics match source data.

**Functional checks (browser):**

- [ ] Open `index.html` from the complete folder with `file://` and optional local server; confirm all panels render.
- [ ] Open standalone HTML; compare content and interactions to multi-file edition.
- [ ] Start learning → Day 001; Next to 002; jump directly to 360; Previous works.
- [ ] Day checkbox updates overall and module progress; refreshed page restores states.
- [ ] Module search, day search, level filter, glossary text/category filter, project cards and mobile nav work.
- [ ] Export progress; import into fresh browser context; invalid/old backup is rejected; reset asks for confirmation.
- [ ] External links open safely in another tab; learn-by-project reference links point to the intended official resource.
- [ ] Copy-code buttons work under supported browser contexts, or provide accessible fallback.
- [ ] Keyboard-only navigation and visible focus; tab order; reduced-motion; screen-reader labels and status.

**Learning quality checks:**

- [ ] At least one person unfamiliar with embeddings can complete early lessons without outside explanation.
- [ ] At least one .NET developer can execute ML.NET classifier lab from supplied sample and reproduce expected metrics.
- [ ] A C# learner can build a model API call and Blazor chat without exposing secrets in a client bundle.
- [ ] A learner can explain and demonstrate embeddings, top-K cosine search, basic document RAG, safe tool calling and evaluation.
- [ ] RAG lab handles no-answer, source links and cross-tenant denial.
- [ ] Agent lab demonstrates authorization, approval and max-step/timeout boundaries.
- [ ] Final capstone architecture, tests and demo are reproducible with cost and privacy disclaimers.

**Packaging checks:**

- [ ] ZIP includes all eight site files plus this handoff if distributing it as the next package.
- [ ] Multi-file ZIP and standalone edition are generated from identical data and version.
- [ ] README setup and links are accurate.
- [ ] No secret files, credentials, private customer data, node_modules, transient build output or unnecessarily bundled licensed binary fonts.
- [ ] Link to tested deliverables and record known limitations without claiming unperformed testing.

## 14. Next developer's recommended execution plan

**Step 1 — Recover exact current release.** Extract complete v2 ZIP, check all eight files, run basic smoke tests; keep original snapshot untouched. Read README, this handoff, and the curriculum data schema.

**Step 2 — Build quality automation before major content changes.** Add a small script validating 360-day structure/references and publishing JSON → JS/standalone/Markdown. Include tests for progress import/export and IDs. Avoid divergent hand-edited copies.

**Step 3 — Prototype enriched teaching on Days 001–015.** Rewrite *only the teaching detail*, not the meaning or IDs: include mini-lesson, C# runnable instructions, sample output, mistakes, tiny quiz, required C# reference. User-test it for simplicity and readability while preserving the blue theme.

**Step 4 — Expand the enriched format phase by phase.** Tackle Days 016–360 in learner dependency order. Add working code/sample project milestones; keep explanations concrete, progressively advanced and Azure/.NET focused.

**Step 5 — Review the entire Microsoft ecosystem coverage.** Use the prioritized audit above; add concepts only where required for complete engineering competency. More modules are fine if justified, but migrate progress safely, update counts, and preserve a clear day-one-to-final path.

**Step 6 — Test real end-to-end projects.** ML.NET → chat → structured output → authorized tools → embeddings → vectors → RAG → agent → MCP → multimodal → production capstone. Document versions, costs, pitfalls and success evidence.

**Step 7 — Retest and deliver.** Browser/desktop/mobile/accessibility, link review, build artifact consistency, updated ZIP, standalone site, editable source, complete Markdown course, changelog and progress-backup compatibility notes.

### Decisions that need explicit consent before major changes

- Shifting the learning website itself to a hosted/cloud-only app, login, database or third-party framework.
- Replacing or materially redesigning the approved Material Blue UI.
- Removing offline capability or requiring subscriptions to follow core lessons.
- Converting all 360 day numbers into fixed calendar dates.
- Changing local progress IDs without a supported migration/backup path.
- Replacing the learner's primary implementation language C# with Python.

## 15. Handoff inventory: what is duplicated below and why

The following appendices deliberately reproduce **all structured learning content**, not merely titles, so this Markdown can be handed to somebody who has not seen the chat or other curriculum files:

- **Appendix A:** All 12 phases and every phase checkpoint.
- **Appendix B:** All 72 modules with goals, concepts, .NET analogies, C# illustration and all **360** daily explanations/tasks/expected outputs/reference links. (This is the true day-by-day full handoff.)
- **Appendix C:** All 18 practical project briefs with inputs, outputs, steps, acceptance criteria and reference links.
- **Appendix D:** All 51 original reference records and their website URLs.
- **Appendix E:** All 208 original glossary entries and short explanations.
- **Appendix F:** All 12 original optional advanced electives.

These appendices describe the *current v2 content as it exists*, including any limitations. Improvements described above are recommendations, **not false claims of implemented lessons or hosted services**.

## Appendix A — Every phase, stage, checkpoint and self-check
### Phase 01: Start from zero: AI in everyday .NET apps — Days 1–30
- **Level:** Beginner
- **Why this stage exists:** Plain English first. Understand exactly when AI is useful—and when ordinary C# is better.
- **Modules:** 01, 02, 03, 04, 05, 06
- **Milestone:** A short README explaining which customer-support features need AI, and which need only C#.
- **Explain in your own words:**
  - Can you explain what ai is (and is not) using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain ai, machine learning and generative ai using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain ai problem types before coding using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 02: Friendly math and reliable data — Days 31–60
- **Level:** Beginner
- **Why this stage exists:** Math you can feel through simple C# arrays and tests, with no calculus prerequisites.
- **Modules:** 07, 08, 09, 10, 11, 12
- **Milestone:** A C# console tool for vector math, probability, data splitting and quality metrics.
- **Explain in your own words:**
  - Can you explain numbers, arrays, vectors and dimensions using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain dot product and cosine similarity using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain simple statistics and probability using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 03: Build real machine learning with ML.NET — Days 61–90
- **Level:** Beginner
- **Why this stage exists:** Train and serve C# models to understand what inference really is.
- **Modules:** 13, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18
- **Milestone:** A tested sentiment REST API plus three small ML.NET experiments.
- **Explain in your own words:**
  - Can you explain ml.net: first binary classifier using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain improve and evaluate your classifier using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain serve a trained model through asp.net core using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 04: Understand neural networks and LLMs — Days 91–120
- **Level:** Beginner
- **Why this stage exists:** Explain the internals in simple words; you do not need to build a transformer.
- **Modules:** 19, 20, 21, 22, 23, 24
- **Milestone:** A visual explanation of how text becomes tokens, vectors, attention and generated text.
- **Explain in your own words:**
  - Can you explain neurons, weights, layers and loss using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain training and backpropagation intuition using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain transformers explained with simple examples using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 05: Make your first C# LLM application — Days 121–150
- **Level:** Beginner
- **Why this stage exists:** Now that the vocabulary is clear, call a real model and build a simple web interface.
- **Modules:** 25, 26, 27, 28, 29, 30
- **Milestone:** A streamed C# chatbot and a basic Blazor chat page, with correct secret handling.
- **Explain in your own words:**
  - Can you explain pick a provider and protect your api key using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain first real model call from c# using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain system, developer, user, assistant and tool messages using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 06: Prompting, JSON and dependable AI endpoints — Days 151–180
- **Level:** Beginner
- **Why this stage exists:** Teach models a clear task and defend your .NET app against unpredictable output.
- **Modules:** 31, 32, 33, 34, 35, 36
- **Milestone:** A support-ticket information extractor with typed JSON and tests.
- **Explain in your own words:**
  - Can you explain write clear prompts without magic tricks using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain context, grounding and context engineering using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain structured output with c# records and json schema using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 07: Connect AI to your C# code and semantic search — Days 181–210
- **Level:** Intermediate
- **Why this stage exists:** First let a model request safe actions. Next turn text into vectors and perform semantic search.
- **Modules:** 37, 38, 39, 40, 41, 42
- **Milestone:** A database-backed order assistant and a product semantic-search API.
- **Explain in your own words:**
  - Can you explain function calling: model suggests, c# executes using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain tool security, authorization and idempotency using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain tool chains and controlled workflows using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 08: Search fundamentals and your first RAG app — Days 211–240
- **Level:** Intermediate
- **Why this stage exists:** Build ingestion and retrieval separately, then combine them into evidence-grounded answers.
- **Modules:** 43, 44, 45, 46, 47, 48
- **Milestone:** A file-upload PDF Q&A assistant with citations and honest “I do not know” answers.
- **Explain in your own words:**
  - Can you explain keyword search, bm25 and why vectors are not enough using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain hybrid search, rrf and reranking using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain read pdf, docx and txt into clean text using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 09: Make search and RAG production-ready — Days 241–270
- **Level:** Intermediate
- **Why this stage exists:** Measure retrieval before adding complexity, then secure, update and optimize the pipeline.
- **Modules:** 49, 50, 51, 52, 53, 54
- **Milestone:** An enterprise RAG system with hybrid search, authorization and meaningful evaluation.
- **Explain in your own words:**
  - Can you explain measure retrieval before tuning prompts using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain better rag queries, reranking and complex questions using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain tenant isolation, search access and secure rag using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 10: Agents, stateful workflows and interoperable tools — Days 271–300
- **Level:** Intermediate
- **Why this stage exists:** Move from one tool request to safely controlled workflows—then learn the agent protocols.
- **Modules:** 55, 56, 57, 58, 59, 60

- **Explain in your own words:**

  - Can you explain conversation, working, semantic and long-term memory using a C# or web-app example?
  - Can you explain microsoft agent framework from basic to useful using a C# or web-app example?

### Phase 11: Beyond chat: multimodal, local and model internals — Days 301–330

## Appendix B — All 72 modules and all 360 daily lessons (unabridged)
**Reading:** Each module has five unique days with the repeating progression `Understand → Try a tiny example → Write C# / build → Test your understanding → Finish and explain`. All original explanatory text, task instructions and expected evidence are preserved below. The C# code snippets are original instructional illustrations, not guarantees of independently compiling projects.

### PHASE 01: Start from zero: AI in everyday .NET apps — Days 1–30
#### Module 01: What AI is (and is not) — Days 001–005 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Create your AI vs normal code decision table
- **Why it matters:** AI predicts or generates from patterns. Unlike an if-statement, output may vary.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** A normal C# service follows your explicit rules; an AI model uses patterns learned earlier.
- **Example:** An order total uses ordinary C#; classifying a customer message may use ML.
- **What to build:** Create your AI vs normal code decision table
- **Concepts introduced:** AI, Model, Deterministic code, Probabilistic output, Inference
- **Supports practical project:** `p0` — AI vs Rules Decision Notebook
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Normal business rules stay normal C#:**
```csharp
static decimal CalculateTotal(decimal price, int quantity)
    => price * quantity;
// This must be exact. An LLM is not needed for multiplication.
// Use AI for tasks like understanding the meaning of free-form text.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 001: AI vs ordinary code** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** AI is not a replacement for every if/else. Use ordinary C# for exact business rules.
- **Do:** List 5 existing web features; mark which need deterministic rules and which might benefit from AI.
- **Definition of done / proof:** A table with 5 decisions
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Day 002: What is a model?** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** A model is a reusable learned function: input in, prediction out.
- **Do:** Draw Request → ASP.NET API → Model → Response. Label where your code validates inputs.
- **Definition of done / proof:** One model call diagram
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Day 003: Prediction is not a fact** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Model answers can be wrong, so verification matters.
- **Do:** Write a tiny C# endpoint that returns a fixed order total; contrast its certainty with sentiment prediction.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Two outcomes compared
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Day 004: When NOT to use AI** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** Exact arithmetic, authorization and transactions belong in code.
- **Do:** Review 6 imagined feature requests and choose rules, ML or LLM for each.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Six justified choices
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Day 005: Explain AI to a teammate** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** Understanding beats memorizing buzzwords.
- **Do:** Add a README: “AI is useful when …; ordinary C# wins when …” and give 3 examples.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Three clear examples in README
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Current module-level recipe (cross-check against day records):**
- Day 1: List 5 existing web features; mark which need deterministic rules and which might benefit from AI.
- Day 2: Draw Request → ASP.NET API → Model → Response. Label where your code validates inputs.
- Day 3: Write a tiny C# endpoint that returns a fixed order total; contrast its certainty with sentiment prediction.
- Day 4: Review 6 imagined feature requests and choose rules, ML or LLM for each.
- Day 5: Add a README: “AI is useful when …; ordinary C# wins when …” and give 3 examples.

**Current module-level verification targets:**
- Day 1: A table with 5 decisions
- Day 2: One model call diagram
- Day 3: Two outcomes compared
- Day 4: Six justified choices
- Day 5: Three clear examples in README

---
#### Module 02: AI, machine learning and generative AI — Days 006–010 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Draw the family tree with .NET examples
- **Why it matters:** AI is the big umbrella; ML learns patterns; generative AI creates new content.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Vehicles → cars → electric cars: related categories, not the same thing.
- **Example:** Spam detection is ML; drafting a support reply is generative AI.
- **What to build:** Draw the family tree with .NET examples
- **Concepts introduced:** AI, Machine learning, Deep learning, Neural networks, Generative AI
- **Supports practical project:** `p0` — AI vs Rules Decision Notebook
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — One C# enum shows different AI tasks:**
```csharp
enum SolutionType { CSharpRule, PredictiveML, GenerativeAI }
Console.WriteLine(SolutionType.CSharpRule); // exact order total
Console.WriteLine(SolutionType.PredictiveML); // predicted delivery delay
Console.WriteLine(SolutionType.GenerativeAI); // draft an email
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 006: AI is the umbrella** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** AI includes search, planning, rules and machine learning.
- **Do:** Draw AI as a large box, placing web recommendation and route planning inside.
- **Definition of done / proof:** One AI category diagram
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Day 007: Machine learning finds patterns** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** ML fits behavior to examples instead of hard-coded conditions.
- **Do:** Compare 3 C# if/else examples with a trained spam detector on paper.
- **Definition of done / proof:** A rules-vs-ML comparison
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Day 008: Deep learning uses many layers** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Deep learning is a subset of ML built from layered neural networks.
- **Do:** Draw a simple network with input, hidden layer and output; do not derive equations.
- **Definition of done / proof:** One annotated network sketch
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Day 009: Generative AI makes content** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** GenAI can generate text, code, audio and images; generation is not validation.
- **Do:** List where a product UI might create content, versus merely classify it.
- **Definition of done / proof:** A 6-row examples table
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Day 010: Choose the right family** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** The technique depends on the business task.
- **Do:** Classify 10 tasks: rule, predictive ML, generative model or retrieval.
- **Definition of done / proof:** 10 checked scenario choices
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Current module-level recipe (cross-check against day records):**
- Day 6: Draw AI as a large box, placing web recommendation and route planning inside.
- Day 7: Compare 3 C# if/else examples with a trained spam detector on paper.
- Day 8: Draw a simple network with input, hidden layer and output; do not derive equations.
- Day 9: List where a product UI might create content, versus merely classify it.
- Day 10: Classify 10 tasks: rule, predictive ML, generative model or retrieval.

**Current module-level verification targets:**
- Day 6: One AI category diagram
- Day 7: A rules-vs-ML comparison
- Day 8: One annotated network sketch
- Day 9: A 6-row examples table
- Day 10: 10 checked scenario choices

#### Module 03: AI problem types before coding — Days 011–015 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build a task-to-model decision guide
- **Why it matters:** Classification, regression, clustering, recommendations and generation solve different problems.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Think of choosing an ASP.NET endpoint: the response type tells you which operation you need.
- **Example:** “Good/Bad” is classification; “delivery ETA” is regression.
- **What to build:** Build a task-to-model decision guide
- **Concepts introduced:** Classification, Regression, Clustering, Recommendation, Generation
- **Supports practical project:** `p0` — AI vs Rules Decision Notebook
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Label your feature request — simple DTO:**
```csharp

var features = new[] {
    new FeatureIdea("Order total", "Rule", "Must be exact"),
    new FeatureIdea("Ticket sentiment", "ML", "Learn from examples"),
    new FeatureIdea("Email draft", "LLM", "Generate text")
};
```

#### Module 08: Dot product and cosine similarity — Days 036–040 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Implement tested cosine similarity in C#
- **Why it matters:** Similarity tells you whether two vectors point in similar directions.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Dot product compares matching array positions; cosine adjusts for length.
- **Example:** [1,0] and [0,1] are not similar in direction.
- **What to build:** Implement tested cosine similarity in C#
- **Concepts introduced:** Dot product, Cosine similarity, Euclidean distance, Normalization, Similarity score
- **Supports practical project:** `p0` — AI vs Rules Decision Notebook
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [pgvector-dotnet](https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector-dotnet) (`pgvec`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Cosine similarity — complete C# method:**
```csharp
static double Cosine(float[] a, float[] b)
{
    if (a.Length == 0 || a.Length != b.Length)
        throw new ArgumentException("Vectors must share a nonzero length");
    double dot = 0, normA = 0, normB = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < a.Length; i++)
    {
        dot += a[i] * b[i];
        normA += a[i] * a[i];
        normB += b[i] * b[i];
    }
    return normA == 0 || normB == 0 ? 0 : dot / Math.Sqrt(normA * normB);
}
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 036: Dot product is multiply-and-add** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Dot product multiplies corresponding values and adds results.
- **Do:** Implement Dot(a,b) with a for loop; calculate [1,2] dot [3,4].
- **Definition of done / proof:** Dot product = 11
- **Read next:** [pgvector-dotnet](https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector-dotnet) (`pgvec`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Day 037: Cosine compares direction** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Cosine similarity is dot divided by the product of lengths.
- **Do:** Implement Cosine(a,b) without calling an AI API.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Cosine method compiled
- **Read next:** [pgvector-dotnet](https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector-dotnet) (`pgvec`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Day 038: Related and unrelated** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Identical directions score near 1; perpendicular vectors score 0.
- **Do:** Compare [1,0] with [2,0] and [0,1].
- **Definition of done / proof:** Expected similarity values
- **Read next:** [pgvector-dotnet](https://github.com/pgvector/pgvector-dotnet) (`pgvec`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Day 039: Edge cases matter** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** Zero vectors and mismatched lengths need explicit behavior.
- **Do:** Add unit tests for zero vector, dimension mismatch and negative values.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Four passing similarity tests

#### Module 09: Simple statistics and probability — Days 041–045 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Create a tiny C# statistics utility
- **Why it matters:** Models use patterns that are uncertain, so probability and sampling help you reason about quality.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** An API latency average hides slow requests; statistics shows the distribution.
- **Example:** 10% of 100 orders = 10 delayed orders on average, not a guarantee.
- **What to build:** Create a tiny C# statistics utility
- **Concepts introduced:** Mean, Median, Probability, Distribution, Sample bias
- **Supports practical project:** `p0` — AI vs Rules Decision Notebook
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Mean and median — easy C#:**
```csharp
float[] values = [2, 3, 3, 4, 100];
Console.WriteLine($"Mean: {values.Average()}");
var sorted = values.Order().ToArray();
Console.WriteLine($"Median: {sorted[sorted.Length/2]}");
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 041: Average, median and spread** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Mean, median and standard deviation describe data differently.
- **Do:** Compute mean and median for order delivery times in a float[].
- **Definition of done / proof:** Mean and median output
- **Read next:** [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 042: What probability means** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Probability is a number between 0 and 1, not a promise of truth.
- **Do:** Simulate 100 random coin flips with Random and count heads.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Observed fraction report
- **Read next:** [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 043: Sampling changes results** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Small or biased samples may mislead.
- **Do:** Compare random 10 reviews against 100 reviews in two quick tests.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Two sample-size observations
- **Read next:** [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 044: Distributions and outliers** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** Unusual values can shift your average.
- **Do:** Add a 100-day delivery outlier; compare mean and median.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Outlier experiment
- **Read next:** [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 045: Explain uncertainty to a user** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** Confidence can be poorly calibrated; avoid claiming certainty.
- **Do:** Write 3 user-friendly statements that do not overpromise probability.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Three safe UI strings
- **Read next:** [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Current module-level recipe (cross-check against day records):**
- Day 41: Compute mean and median for order delivery times in a float[].
- Day 42: Simulate 100 random coin flips with Random and count heads.
- Day 43: Compare random 10 reviews against 100 reviews in two quick tests.
- Day 44: Add a 100-day delivery outlier; compare mean and median.
- Day 45: Write 3 user-friendly statements that do not overpromise probability.

#### Module 10: Training, validation, testing and leakage — Days 046–050 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build a deterministic dataset splitter
- **Why it matters:** A model must be tested on examples it did not memorize.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** A student practicing from the answer sheet may score well but learn nothing.
- **Example:** Training accuracy 99% and test accuracy 55% is a red flag.
- **What to build:** Build a deterministic dataset splitter
- **Concepts introduced:** Training split, Validation split, Test split, Overfitting, Leakage
- **Supports practical project:** `p0` — AI vs Rules Decision Notebook
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Dataset split — concept:**
```csharp
// Keep separate train, validation and test examples.
// Never copy a labeled answer into model input features.
// Compare model predictions on unseen TEST data only.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 046: Why split data?** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Training and evaluation need separate examples.
- **Do:** Split a list of 100 sample IDs into 80 train and 20 test items.
- **Definition of done / proof:** 80/20 split report
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Day 047: Validation is for choices** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Validation helps compare settings without using your final test set.
- **Do:** Split 100 IDs into 70 train, 15 validation and 15 test.
- **Definition of done / proof:** 70/15/15 split
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Day 048: What overfitting looks like** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Memorizing training examples can harm performance on new inputs.
- **Do:** Write a simple “lookup table” predictor that fails on unknown review text.
- **Definition of done / proof:** One deliberately overfit predictor
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Day 049: Underfitting is too simple** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** A model that cannot learn patterns performs poorly everywhere.
- **Do:** Use a constant predictor and compare correct vs wrong labels.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Constant-baseline results
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Day 050: Prevent leaks and duplicate rows** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** Duplicates across splits make accuracy look better than it is.
- **Do:** Add a check that train/test IDs do not overlap; write a test.
- **Definition of done / proof:** No-overlap xUnit test
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Current module-level recipe (cross-check against day records):**

- Day 47: Split 100 IDs into 70 train, 15 validation and 15 test.

#### Module 11: Understand classifier metrics — Days 051–055 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Print a confusion matrix and F1 in C#
- **Why it matters:** Accuracy alone hides mistakes, especially when most cases have the same label.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** A smoke alarm is evaluated on missed fires AND false alarms.
- **Example:** A model claiming “no fraud” can be 99% accurate on rare fraud cases.
- **What to build:** Print a confusion matrix and F1 in C#
- **Concepts introduced:** Confusion matrix, Precision, Recall, F1 score, Accuracy
- **Supports practical project:** `p0` — AI vs Rules Decision Notebook
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Precision / Recall / F1 — tiny C#:**
```csharp
double tp = 8, fp = 2, fn = 4;
double precision = tp / (tp + fp);
double recall = tp / (tp + fn);
double f1 = 2 * precision * recall / (precision + recall);
Console.WriteLine($"Precision={precision:F2}, Recall={recall:F2}, F1={f1:F2}");
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 051: Four prediction outcomes** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** TP, TN, FP and FN record right/wrong classifications.
- **Do:** Create 8 truth/prediction pairs and count TP,TN,FP,FN.
- **Definition of done / proof:** 2x2 confusion matrix
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Day 052: Precision means false alarm control** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** High precision means predicted positives are usually right.
- **Do:** Compute precision = TP/(TP+FP) for your 8 cases.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Precision calculation
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Day 053: Recall means missed-case control** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** High recall means you find most actual positives.
- **Do:** Compute recall = TP/(TP+FN) and discuss missed urgent tickets.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Recall calculation
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Day 054: F1 balances precision and recall** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** F1 summarizes precision/recall tradeoff; accuracy is different.
- **Do:** Add F1 and accuracy methods with divide-by-zero handling.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Metric helper tests
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Day 055: Pick a metric for the product** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** Business cost determines whether false positives or negatives are worse.
- **Do:** Write why recall may matter for fraud and precision for auto-refund approvals.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Two metric decisions
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`)

**Current module-level recipe (cross-check against day records):**
- Day 51: Create 8 truth/prediction pairs and count TP,TN,FP,FN.
- Day 52: Compute precision = TP/(TP+FP) for your 8 cases.
- Day 53: Compute recall = TP/(TP+FN) and discuss missed urgent tickets.
- Day 54: Add F1 and accuracy methods with divide-by-zero handling.
- Day 55: Write why recall may matter for fraud and precision for auto-refund approvals.

**Current module-level verification targets:**
- Day 51: 2x2 confusion matrix
- Day 52: Precision calculation
- Day 53: Recall calculation
- Day 54: Metric helper tests
- Day 55: Two metric decisions

#### Module 12: Bias, fairness, privacy and good decisions — Days 056–060 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Write a responsible-use checklist for your ML API
- **Why it matters:** Data and model errors can affect people, so responsible AI starts early.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** A C# feature is not “fair” just because it compiles.
- **Example:** A support model might work worse on short messages or another language.
- **What to build:** Write a responsible-use checklist for your ML API
- **Concepts introduced:** Fairness, Bias, Privacy, Human oversight, Model card
- **Supports practical project:** `p0` — AI vs Rules Decision Notebook
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Responsible AI resources](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/ai/responsible-ai) (`privacy`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications](https://genai.owasp.org/llm-top-10/) (`owasp`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Begin with synthetic, non-identifying data:**
```csharp
public record TestReview(string Text, string Language);
var reviews = new[] {
    new TestReview("Works well", "en"),
    new TestReview("Très bon", "fr")
};
// Compare quality by language. Never train on private customer data casually.
```

#### Module 14: Improve and evaluate your classifier — Days 066–070 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Compare baseline and improved ML.NET pipelines
- **Why it matters:** Training is only half the job; you need honest testing and error inspection.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Just like unit tests, evaluate scenarios the model has not seen.
- **Example:** “Not terrible” is harder than “Fantastic!” for naive sentiment.
- **What to build:** Compare baseline and improved ML.NET pipelines
- **Concepts introduced:** TrainTestSplit, BinaryClassification.Evaluate, Baseline, Error analysis, F1 score
- **Supports practical project:** `p0` — AI vs Rules Decision Notebook
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET classification](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/tutorials/sentiment-analysis) (`mldocs`); [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — A test set should not be your training set:**
```csharp
var trainIds = new HashSet<int>(Enumerable.Range(1, 80));
var testIds = new HashSet<int>(Enumerable.Range(81, 20));
Console.WriteLine(trainIds.Overlaps(testIds)); // False
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 066: Hold out unseen data** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Training and test rows must be separate.
- **Do:** Use TrainTestSplit and record exact row counts.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Split counts in README

**Day 068: Find wrong predictions** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Inspect examples that were classified incorrectly.

#### Module 15: Serve a trained model through ASP.NET Core — Days 071–075 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build your ML.NET Sentiment REST API
- **Why it matters:** An API should load a trained model and safely score input.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Treat a model as a dependency injected service behind a DTO and endpoint.
- **Example:** POST /api/sentiment returns label and confidence-like score.
- **What to build:** Build your ML.NET Sentiment REST API
- **Concepts introduced:** Model serving, ASP.NET Core API, Dependency injection, Input validation, Inference
- **Supports practical project:** `p1` — ML.NET Sentiment REST API
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — Minimal APIs](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/minimal-apis) (`minimal`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — ASP.NET API — safe skeleton:**
```csharp
// IClassifier is an application-owned interface.
app.MapPost("/api/sentiment", (ReviewRequest request, IClassifier classifier) =>
{
    if (string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(request.Text))
        return Results.BadRequest("Text is required");
    return Results.Ok(classifier.Predict(request.Text));
});
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 071: Create your prediction API** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** The Web API receives text and returns typed JSON.

#### Module 16: Regression with ML.NET — Days 076–080 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build a delivery ETA predictor
- **Why it matters:** Regression predicts a number such as a forecast or price.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** It is like decimal-returning API logic, except the parameters are learned.
- **Example:** Predict shipping duration from item count, distance and weight.
- **What to build:** Build a delivery ETA predictor
- **Concepts introduced:** Regression, Continuous label, MAE, RMSE, Feature pipeline
- **Supports practical project:** `p2` — Delivery ETA Predictor
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Regression predicts a number:**
```csharp
public record DeliveryFeatures(float DistanceKm, float WeightKg);
public record DeliveryPrediction(float EstimatedDays);
// Example target is 2.4 days — not a True/False class label.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 076: Define a numeric target** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Regression has a continuous numeric label.

- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`)

- **Understand:** Use an ML.NET regression trainer and feature concatenation.
- **Do:** Adapt the official regression sample to your DeliveryRow type.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Trained ETA model
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`); [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`)

#### Module 17: Clustering and recommendations in .NET — Days 081–085 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Make two small recommendation experiments
- **Why it matters:** Not all ML is trained from correct labels.

- **Concepts introduced:** Unsupervised learning, K-means, Collaborative filtering, Cold start, Ranking
- **Supports practical project:** `p2` — Delivery ETA Predictor
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.

```csharp
public record ProductPoint(string Name, float Price, float WeightKg);

    new("Mouse", 20, .2f), new("Laptop", 900, 1.6f)
];
// A clustering algorithm would group similar points. This is the data.
```
