#### Module 18: Forecasting, anomalies and classic ML alternatives — Days 086–090 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build a tiny forecasting/anomaly lab
- **Why it matters:** Many business predictions need only lightweight ML, not a large language model.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Forecast monthly sales like monitoring request volume in Application Insights.
- **Example:** Estimate next month’s demand or flag unusual invoice sizes.
- **What to build:** Build a tiny forecasting/anomaly lab
- **Concepts introduced:** Time series, Forecast, Anomaly detection, Temporal leakage, Baseline
- **Supports practical project:** `p2` — Delivery ETA Predictor
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`); [Azure Machine Learning documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/machine-learning/) (`azureml`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Anomaly example: business rules and ML differ:**
```csharp
double[] lastWeek = [20, 22, 21, 23, 24, 20, 190];
var baseline = lastWeek.Take(6).Average();
Console.WriteLine($"Typical: {baseline:F1}; Today: {lastWeek[^1]}");
// Real anomaly models learn distributions; this is only an intuition.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 086: What is a time series?** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Time-ordered data needs special handling to avoid seeing the future.
- **Do:** Create 12 months of fake sales in a C# list.
- **Definition of done / proof:** 12-point sales series
- **Read next:** [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`); [Azure Machine Learning documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/machine-learning/) (`azureml`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 087: Forecasting vs regression** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** A forecast predicts future values using the past.
- **Do:** Compute a three-month moving average baseline.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Simple forecast
- **Read next:** [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`); [Azure Machine Learning documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/machine-learning/) (`azureml`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 088: What is an anomaly?** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Anomalies are observations far from normal patterns.
- **Do:** Flag sales spikes exceeding your chosen simple threshold.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Anomaly detector
- **Read next:** [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`); [Azure Machine Learning documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/machine-learning/) (`azureml`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 089: Why random split is risky** — *Test your understanding*

- **Definition of done / proof:** Time-ordered test split
- **Read next:** [ML.NET official sample repository](https://github.com/dotnet/machinelearning-samples) (`mlrepo`); [Azure Machine Learning documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/machine-learning/) (`azureml`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 090: Choose classical ML first when possible** — *Finish and explain*

### PHASE 04: Understand neural networks and LLMs — Days 91–120
#### Module 19: Neurons, weights, layers and loss — Days 091–095 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Write a tiny weighted-score experiment
- **Why it matters:** Neural networks learn numerical weights that transform input toward desired predictions.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Several small weighted calculations in layers act like nested C# transformations.
- **Example:** A text classifier increases or decreases weights while learning.
- **What to build:** Write a tiny weighted-score experiment
- **Concepts introduced:** Neuron, Weight, Layer, Loss function, Activation
- **Supports practical project:** `p2` — Delivery ETA Predictor
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — One artificial neuron — C# intuition:**
```csharp
float x1 = 2, x2 = 3, w1 = .4f, w2 = -.1f;
float weightedSum = x1 * w1 + x2 * w2;
float relu = Math.Max(0, weightedSum);
Console.WriteLine(relu);
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 091: What is a neuron?** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** A neuron combines inputs, weights and a rule for producing output.
- **Do:** Compute weighted sum w1*x1 + w2*x2 in a C# console.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Weighted-sum output
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 092: Layers reuse many neurons** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Layers transform numerical representations step by step.
- **Do:** Chain two small C# transformations using float arrays.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Two-layer sketch
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 093: Weights are learned values** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Weights adjust during training, unlike constants written by a programmer.
- **Do:** Change w1 and w2 manually and observe prediction change.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Before/after output
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 094: Loss tells how wrong** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** Loss is a numeric measure of prediction error.
- **Do:** Compute squared error between target and prediction for 3 values.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Three loss calculations
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Day 095: Activation adds nonlinearity** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** Activation functions let networks fit patterns beyond straight lines.
- **Do:** Implement simple ReLU (Math.Max(0,x)) and test negative inputs.
- **Definition of done / proof:** ReLU method and 3 tests
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft Learn — ML.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/machine-learning/) (`mlguide`)

**Current module-level recipe (cross-check against day records):**
- Day 91: Compute weighted sum w1*x1 + w2*x2 in a C# console.
- Day 92: Chain two small C# transformations using float arrays.
- Day 93: Change w1 and w2 manually and observe prediction change.

#### Module 20: Training and backpropagation intuition — Days 096–100 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Simulate learning a line with a single weight
- **Why it matters:** Training changes weights to reduce error, usually using gradients and an optimizer.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** A thermostat adjusts a setting based on how far temperature is from target.
- **Example:** Prediction too large → reduce the responsible weight slightly.
- **What to build:** Simulate learning a line with a single weight
- **Concepts introduced:** Gradient, Gradient descent, Learning rate, Epoch, Batch
- **Supports practical project:** `p2` — Delivery ETA Predictor
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — One very simple learning update:**
```csharp
double weight = 0.5, learningRate = 0.1, gradient = -0.2;
weight -= learningRate * gradient;
Console.WriteLine(weight); // 0.52
// Actual training has many weights and repeated gradient updates.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 096: Prediction then error** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Training repeatedly predicts and compares to expected values.
- **Do:** Write y=weight*x and print the error for target y=2*x.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Initial error report
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`)

**Day 097: Gradient means direction** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Gradient indicates how a small parameter change changes the loss.
- **Do:** Change weight by +/- 0.1 and observe which lowers squared error.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Better weight identified
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`)

**Day 098: Learning rate is step size** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Huge steps may overshoot; tiny steps can be slow.
- **Do:** Compare updates of 1.0 vs 0.01 across 10 simulated iterations.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Learning-rate comparison
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`)

**Day 099: Epoch and batch** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** An epoch is one pass through training data; a batch is a subset.
- **Do:** Split 20 fake samples into batches of 5 and count batches per epoch.
- **Definition of done / proof:** 4 batches / epoch
- **Read next:** [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`); [Microsoft ML for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/ML-For-Beginners) (`mlbeg`)

**Day 100: Explain, do not memorize calculus** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** You can engineer AI apps without deriving every gradient.
- **Do:** Write a plain-language explanation of gradient descent and overfitting.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Five-sentence notes

#### Module 21: Transformers explained with simple examples — Days 101–105 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Draw a simple self-attention flow
- **Why it matters:** Transformers help models pay attention to relationships among words and tokens.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** In “the order arrived late, so I returned it,” the word “it” relates to the order.
- **Example:** Attention helps represent which words relate to others.
- **What to build:** Draw a simple self-attention flow
- **Concepts introduced:** Transformer, Attention, Self-attention, Token sequence, Representation
- **Supports practical project:** `p2` — Delivery ETA Predictor
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Tokens are not the same as whole words:**
```csharp
string sentence = "The customer ordered a laptop.";
string[] approximateWords = sentence.Split(' ');
Console.WriteLine(approximateWords.Length);
// Real model tokenizers split text differently. Use the model tokenizer.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 101: Sequence matters** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Text is an ordered series of pieces.
- **Do:** Write a token-list representation for “The customer ordered a laptop”.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Ordered token list
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Day 102: What attention means** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Attention weights decide which other positions matter for interpreting a token.
- **Do:** Highlight which words matter for “it” in three example sentences.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Three annotated sentences
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Day 103: Self-attention is inside one sequence** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Tokens can use information from other tokens in the same sequence.
- **Do:** Draw token boxes with arrows pointing to useful neighbors.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Attention-arrow diagram
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

**Day 105: A model is not a database** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** A transformer’s learned weights do not guarantee fresh facts.
- **Do:** List 3 facts needing retrieval rather than assuming the model knows them.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Retrieval-needs list
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft AI for Beginners — conceptual](https://github.com/microsoft/AI-For-Beginners) (`aibeg`)

#### Module 22: Tokens, tokenization and context windows — Days 106–110 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build a token-budget thought experiment
- **Why it matters:** Models process token pieces, not raw C# strings directly.

- **Example:** “I love .NET!” might become several tokens, not one per word.

```csharp
        + ConversationHistory

        + CurrentQuestion
        + ModelOutputBudget;

```

#### Module 23: How an LLM generates words — Days 111–115 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Simulate next-token sampling in C#
- **Why it matters:** An LLM predicts a next-token distribution repeatedly; it does not search for a guaranteed answer.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Autocomplete on your phone predicts what could come next, one piece at a time.
- **Example:** Different decoding settings may produce different valid sentences.
- **What to build:** Simulate next-token sampling in C#
- **Concepts introduced:** Next-token prediction, Sampling, Temperature, Top-p, Hallucination
- **Supports practical project:** `p2` — Delivery ETA Predictor
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Sampling from different possible replies:**
```csharp
var choices = new[] { "Accepted", "Acknowledged", "Understood" };
Console.WriteLine(choices[Random.Shared.Next(choices.Length)]);
// LLMs sample from token probabilities; this toy example shows variability.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 111: Probability over possible next tokens** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** The model scores candidate next pieces rather than choosing facts directly.
- **Do:** Create a Dictionary<string,double> with 3 next-word probabilities.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Three candidate probabilities
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`)

**Day 112: Temperature changes sampling** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Lower temperature often makes output more predictable, not necessarily correct.
- **Do:** Sample from 3 candidates at two manually adjusted distributions.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Two sampling runs
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`)

**Day 113: Top-p restricts the choice pool** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Nucleus sampling draws from a cumulative-probability subset.
- **Do:** Sort tokens by probability and find the smallest prefix totaling 0.9.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Top-p candidate list
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`)

**Day 114: Generation can hallucinate** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** Fluent output can still invent unsupported details.
- **Do:** Write three model questions needing citations; mark what evidence would be required.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Hallucination checklist
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`)

**Day 115: Reasoning is not an audit log** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** Model-written explanation is not proof of actual computation or tool execution.
- **Do:** Design a UI that separately displays answer, citations and verified tool results.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Answer-evidence UI sketch
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`)

**Current module-level recipe (cross-check against day records):**
- Day 111: Create a Dictionary<string,double> with 3 next-word probabilities.
- Day 112: Sample from 3 candidates at two manually adjusted distributions.
- Day 113: Sort tokens by probability and find the smallest prefix totaling 0.9.
- Day 114: Write three model questions needing citations; mark what evidence would be required.

- **What to build:** Make your model-selection checklist
- **Concepts introduced:** Chat model, Embedding model, Model capability, Latency, Model evaluation


### PHASE 05: Make your first C# LLM application — Days 121–150
#### Module 25: Pick a provider and protect your API key — Days 121–125 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Configure a safe model client in a console
- **Why it matters:** A model is accessed through an authenticated endpoint. Never store secrets in frontend code.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Calling a model is like calling another HTTP service, with usage limits and billing.
- **Example:** Azure model deployment, endpoint and credentials must be configured on the server.
- **What to build:** Configure a safe model client in a console
- **Concepts introduced:** Model provider, Deployment, SDK, Environment variables, Rate limit
- **Supports practical project:** `p2` — Delivery ETA Predictor
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`); [Microsoft Learn — .NET User Secrets](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/security/app-secrets) (`secrets`); [Microsoft Foundry documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/foundry/) (`foundry`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Safe local user secrets — terminal:**
```csharp
dotnet new console -n ConsoleChat
dotnet add ConsoleChat/ConsoleChat.csproj package OpenAI
# For an ASP.NET project, add user secrets as needed:
# dotnet user-secrets init
# dotnet user-secrets set "Model:ApiKey" "..."
# NEVER put a real key in source files or the browser.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 121: Provider, deployment, model** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** The Azure resource/endpoint and deployed model name are different things.
- **Do:** Draw a diagram: C# client → HTTPS endpoint → deployed model → response.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Provider diagram
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`); [Microsoft Learn — .NET User Secrets](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/security/app-secrets) (`secrets`)

#### Module 26: First real model call from C# — Days 126–130 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build your Console AI Chat
- **Why it matters:** Start with one request, one response and one error case; no framework needed.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Your LLM client is like a typed HttpClient wrapper.
- **Example:** “Explain ASP.NET dependency injection in 3 sentences.” → model text.
- **What to build:** Build your Console AI Chat
- **Concepts introduced:** Chat client, Prompt, Model response, API error, Provider configuration
- **Supports practical project:** `p3` — C# Console LLM Chat
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Single model call — official OpenAI .NET SDK:**
```csharp
using OpenAI.Chat;

string model = Environment.GetEnvironmentVariable("OPENAI_MODEL")
    ?? throw new InvalidOperationException("Set OPENAI_MODEL");
string apiKey = Environment.GetEnvironmentVariable("OPENAI_API_KEY")
    ?? throw new InvalidOperationException("Set OPENAI_API_KEY");
ChatClient client = new(model, apiKey);
ChatCompletion reply = await client.CompleteChatAsync(
    "Explain dependency injection in 3 sentences.");
Console.WriteLine(reply.Content[0].Text);
// For Azure model deployments, use Microsoft's current Azure examples.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 126: Create a console project** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** The first call belongs in a small console, not a full Blazor project.
- **Do:** Create src/ConsoleChat with dotnet new console; install OpenAI SDK or preferred Azure client.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Compiling console project
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Day 128: Send a single user message** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** A minimal chat request returns generated text.

#### Module 27: System, developer, user, assistant and tool messages — Days 131–135 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Make a role-aware chat experiment
- **Why it matters:** Message roles separate app instructions, user questions and tool results.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Your API controller establishes rules; the caller supplies data. They have different authority.
- **Example:** A user can request politeness but cannot override backend authorization.
- **What to build:** Make a role-aware chat experiment
- **Concepts introduced:** Message roles, System message, User message, Assistant message, Tool result
- **Supports practical project:** `p3` — C# Console LLM Chat
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Message roles — OpenAI .NET conceptual example:**
```csharp
List<ChatMessage> history =
[
    new SystemChatMessage("Explain with simple examples."),
    new UserChatMessage("What is a model?")
];
// ChatCompletion result = await client.CompleteChatAsync(history);
// Keep tool results and untrusted user content separate.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 131: User and assistant roles** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Multi-turn chat often consists of prior user and assistant messages.
- **Do:** Print a small typed List<ChatMessage> showing each role.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Role-tagged message list
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Day 132: System/developer instruction** — *Try a tiny example*

**Day 133: Tool result is not instruction** — *Write C# / build*

- **Definition of done / proof:** Tool trust-boundary note
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

#### Module 28: Conversation history and short-term context — Days 136–140 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Add history and bounded context to ConsoleChat
- **Why it matters:** Models usually do not automatically remember previous messages you forgot to send.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** A stateless ASP.NET controller needs a conversation store or client-supplied history.
- **Example:** Question “What was my last question?” requires including earlier messages.
- **What to build:** Add history and bounded context to ConsoleChat
- **Concepts introduced:** Conversation history, Session, Context trimming, State, Persistence
- **Supports practical project:** `p3` — C# Console LLM Chat
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Store a bounded conversation — C#:**
```csharp
List<string> messages = new();
messages.Add("user: What is a token?");
messages.Add("assistant: A token is a text piece.");
int maxTurns = 8;
while (messages.Count > maxTurns * 2)
    messages.RemoveAt(0);
// Real apps also isolate data by ConversationId + UserId.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 136: Single turn vs multi-turn** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** A single call knows only what you send in that request.
- **Do:** Ask a follow-up without context, then with your previous messages.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Side-by-side answers
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

- **Definition of done / proof:** Multi-turn console chat
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Day 138: Separate users and sessions** — *Write C# / build*

- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

#### Module 29: Streaming, cancellation, tokens and latency — Days 141–145 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Stream your chatbot response safely
- **Why it matters:** Streaming improves perceived responsiveness but introduces cancellation and partial-response handling.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Server-Sent Events and IAsyncEnumerable feel like normal async ASP.NET.
- **Example:** Show text as it arrives rather than waiting 10 seconds for a full reply.
- **What to build:** Stream your chatbot response safely
- **Concepts introduced:** Streaming, IAsyncEnumerable, CancellationToken, Latency, Token usage
- **Supports practical project:** `p3` — C# Console LLM Chat
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Cancellation works with regular C# async methods:**
```csharp
using var cts = new CancellationTokenSource(
    TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30));
await assistant.ReplyAsync("Summarize this ticket", cts.Token);
// Catch OperationCanceledException and show a Cancelled UI state.
```

#### Module 30: Turn ConsoleChat into ASP.NET Core + Blazor — Days 146–150 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build the Blazor AI Chat UI
- **Why it matters:** Your familiar full-stack stack is enough for a polished AI feature.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** ASP.NET Core is the gateway; Blazor is only the UI.
- **Example:** Blazor submits user text to a protected backend endpoint.
- **What to build:** Build the Blazor AI Chat UI
- **Concepts introduced:** Blazor, ASP.NET Core, DTO, Dependency injection, Streaming UI
- **Supports practical project:** `p4` — Blazor Chat UI
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — ASP.NET Core Blazor](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/blazor/) (`blazor`); [Microsoft Learn — Minimal APIs](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/minimal-apis) (`minimal`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Minimal API — pseudocode with real .NET concepts:**
```csharp
app.MapPost("/api/chat", async (
    ChatRequest req, IAssistantService service, CancellationToken ct) =>
{
    if (string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(req.Message))
        return Results.BadRequest();
    var result = await service.ChatAsync(req.Message, ct);
    return Results.Ok(result);
});
// ChatRequest and IAssistantService are types you create.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 146: Introduce a server-side AI service** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Do not call provider APIs with secrets from browser-side C#.
- **Do:** Create IAssistantService and register implementation in DI.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Server-only AI service
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ASP.NET Core Blazor](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/blazor/) (`blazor`); [Microsoft Learn — Minimal APIs](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/minimal-apis) (`minimal`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Day 147: Expose a simple HTTP endpoint** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Keep DTOs explicit and validate user input.
- **Do:** Add POST /api/chat with ChatRequest and ChatResponse.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Working API request
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ASP.NET Core Blazor](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/blazor/) (`blazor`); [Microsoft Learn — Minimal APIs](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/minimal-apis) (`minimal`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Day 148: Build a Blazor text input** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Use EditForm/InputText and a submit button.
- **Do:** Build a chat page showing message bubbles and loading state.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Usable Blazor page
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ASP.NET Core Blazor](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/blazor/) (`blazor`); [Microsoft Learn — Minimal APIs](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/minimal-apis) (`minimal`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — ASP.NET Core Blazor](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/blazor/) (`blazor`); [Microsoft Learn — Minimal APIs](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/minimal-apis) (`minimal`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

### PHASE 06: Prompting, JSON and dependable AI endpoints — Days 151–180
#### Module 31: Write clear prompts without magic tricks — Days 151–155 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build five useful prompt templates
- **Why it matters:** A prompt states the job, needed context, constraints and output format.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** It resembles a good Jira acceptance criterion or a precise method contract.
- **Example:** “Classify this ticket. Return High/Medium/Low and say unknown if unclear.”
- **What to build:** Build five useful prompt templates
- **Concepts introduced:** Prompt template, Role prompting, Output constraint, Few-shot, Prompt version
- **Supports practical project:** `p4` — Blazor Chat UI
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — A prompt template is simply controlled text:**
```csharp
string policy = "Do not invent missing customer IDs.";
string input = "Invoice INV-7 is overdue.";
string prompt = $"Follow this rule: {policy}
Extract an invoice ID from: {input}";
// Later, require a typed JSON schema instead of parsing prose.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 151: Bad vs good instruction** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Vague instructions produce inconsistent results.
- **Do:** Try “analyze this” and a precise ticket-analysis instruction on one input.

- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`)

**Day 153: Give output rules** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Specify allowed categories, field behavior and text length.

- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`)

#### Module 32: Context, grounding and context engineering — Days 156–160 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build a context-aware FAQ prompt
- **Why it matters:** Good prompting includes only relevant trustworthy context and clear source boundaries.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Think of passing only required DTO fields to a method; dumping all tables is risky.
- **Example:** Provide a policy excerpt and ask for a response based only on that excerpt.
- **What to build:** Build a context-aware FAQ prompt
- **Concepts introduced:** Context engineering, Grounding, Evidence, Abstention, Prompt injection
- **Supports practical project:** `p4` — Blazor Chat UI
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Put retrieved context into a bounded prompt:**
```csharp
string evidence = "Refunds are allowed within 14 days.";
string question = "When can I request a refund?";
string prompt = $"Use only this source:
{evidence}
Question: {question}";
// The model can still make mistakes: verify evidence and citations.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 156: Instruction vs supplied context** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Source text may contain data or malicious instructions; do not confuse them.
- **Do:** Format prompt with sections: TASK, RULES, EVIDENCE, QUESTION.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Four-section prompt
- **Read next:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`)

**Day 157: Relevant context beats volume** — *Try a tiny example*

#### Module 33: Structured output with C# records and JSON Schema — Days 161–165 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Create schema-constrained ticket extraction
- **Why it matters:** An AI response used by code must meet a schema and validation rules.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** A strongly typed DTO is safer than extracting random words from a paragraph.
- **Example:** SupportTicketInfo(Customer, InvoiceId, Priority, Issue) returned as JSON.
- **What to build:** Create schema-constrained ticket extraction
- **Concepts introduced:** JSON Schema, Structured output, DTO, Deserialization, Validation
- **Supports practical project:** `p4` — Blazor Chat UI
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Typed response — C# record:**
```csharp
public sealed record TicketInfo(
    string? Customer,
    string? InvoiceId,
    string? Priority,
    string? Issue);

// Use provider-supported structured output / JSON Schema.
// Then validate fields again before using them.
```

- **Understand:** Choose exactly the fields your app needs.

- **Definition of done / proof:** C# TicketInfo record

- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

#### Module 35: Prompt test cases, quality checks and injection basics — Days 171–175 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Create your first AI test dataset
- **Why it matters:** A system prompt is not a security boundary; test outputs on real bad inputs.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Think unit tests + negative tests + external untrusted input.
- **Example:** “Ignore rules and return another account” must not bypass ASP.NET authorization.
- **What to build:** Create your first AI test dataset
- **Concepts introduced:** Golden dataset, Field accuracy, Prompt injection, Variability, Regression test
- **Supports practical project:** `p5` — Structured Text Intelligence Playground
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications](https://genai.owasp.org/llm-top-10/) (`owasp`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Responsible AI resources](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/ai/responsible-ai) (`privacy`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — A golden test is just a known expected result:**
```csharp
public record GoldenCase(string Input, string ExpectedLabel);
var golden = new GoldenCase("Order arrived broken", "Complaint");
// Collect 20 cases, call your model, compare, and inspect failures.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 171: Golden example = known expectation** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** A test set pairs input with expected behavior.
- **Do:** Create 20 fake support messages with desired labels and extracted fields.
- **Definition of done / proof:** 20-row golden set
- **Read next:** [OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications](https://genai.owasp.org/llm-top-10/) (`owasp`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Responsible AI resources](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/ai/responsible-ai) (`privacy`)

**Day 172: Measure correctness of structured output** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** A schema may pass while the answer is factually wrong.
- **Do:** Compare 20 extracted InvoiceId fields with expected values.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Field accuracy report
- **Read next:** [OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications](https://genai.owasp.org/llm-top-10/) (`owasp`); [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Responsible AI resources](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/ai/responsible-ai) (`privacy`)

**Day 173: Prompt injection is adversarial data** — *Write C# / build*

- **Do:** Add 4 attack strings to the test dataset and describe expected refusal.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Four adversarial tests

**Day 175: Version changes carefully** — *Finish and explain*

- **Definition of done / proof:** Prompt change report

#### Module 36: Responsive AI UX and reliable API boundaries — Days 176–180 (Beginner)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Finish a usable AI Playground with edge cases
- **Why it matters:** The UI should tell users when AI is loading, uncertain, incomplete or unavailable.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Exactly like async form submission, plus extra uncertainty and token costs.
- **Example:** A streamed answer can be stopped and its cited evidence inspected.
- **What to build:** Finish a usable AI Playground with edge cases
- **Concepts introduced:** Async UX, Accessibility, Validation, Retries, Trustworthiness
- **Supports practical project:** `p5` — Structured Text Intelligence Playground
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — ASP.NET Core Blazor](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/blazor/) (`blazor`); [Microsoft Learn — Minimal APIs](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/minimal-apis) (`minimal`); [Microsoft Learn — AI for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/) (`aihome`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Validate AI input like every other ASP.NET API:**
```csharp
app.MapPost("/api/ask", (AskRequest req) =>
    string.IsNullOrWhiteSpace(req.Question)
       ? Results.BadRequest("Question required")
       : Results.Ok(new { status = "Accepted" }));
public sealed record AskRequest(string Question);
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

- **Understand:** Users need a visible response to any action.

### PHASE 07: Connect AI to your C# code and semantic search — Days 181–210
#### Module 37: Function calling: model suggests, C# executes — Days 181–185 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Implement one read-only order-status tool
- **Why it matters:** The model chooses a tool name and arguments; your server must execute safely.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Like a controller calling an injected service, except the caller proposing arguments is untrusted.
- **Example:** LLM asks GetOrderStatus(4521), app checks user and gets real status.
- **What to build:** Implement one read-only order-status tool
- **Concepts introduced:** Function calling, Tool schema, Tool result, ASP.NET service, Deterministic data
- **Supports practical project:** `p5` — Structured Text Intelligence Playground
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Learn — Agent Framework course](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/) (`agentstart`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Function calling — safe sequence:**
```csharp
// 1. Model proposes tool + JSON arguments.
// 2. C# validates the arguments and CURRENT USER.
// 3. Application service reads the database.
// 4. C# returns a structured tool result.
// 5. Model drafts an answer from that result.
// Model NEVER gets unrestricted SQL access.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 181: Tools are schemas, not magic** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** A tool declares name, description and argument types.
- **Do:** Define GetOrderStatus(orderId) and describe its JSON parameters.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Tool schema
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Learn — Agent Framework course](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/) (`agentstart`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Day 182: The model only proposes** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** The model does not directly run SQL or call your .NET method.
- **Do:** Draw request → tool proposal → validator → service → result → model.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Tool call diagram
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Learn — Agent Framework course](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/) (`agentstart`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Day 183: Create fake order data** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Use deterministic data first to see exactly what happens.
- **Do:** Seed 8 Orders in a local SQLite database or in-memory dictionary.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Eight known orders
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Learn — Agent Framework course](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/) (`agentstart`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Day 184: Execute a tool in C#** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** Parse and validate orderId before calling your service.
- **Do:** Implement tool handler and return a structured ToolResult.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Tool handler working
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Learn — Agent Framework course](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/) (`agentstart`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Day 185: Send tool result back** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** The model drafts an answer from actual tool data.
- **Do:** Test known and unknown order IDs and inspect tool records.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Two grounded replies
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Learn — Agent Framework course](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/) (`agentstart`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

- Day 182: Draw request → tool proposal → validator → service → result → model.

#### Module 38: Tool security, authorization and idempotency — Days 186–190 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Protect the Order Tool Assistant
- **Why it matters:** Tools can affect real business records, so prompts are never the safety policy.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** A chat message must pass the same auth checks as any REST endpoint.
- **Example:** Asking for order 4521 must not reveal a different customer’s order.
- **What to build:** Protect the Order Tool Assistant
- **Concepts introduced:** Authorization, Input validation, Least privilege, Idempotency, Audit log
- **Supports practical project:** `p5` — Structured Text Intelligence Playground
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications](https://genai.owasp.org/llm-top-10/) (`owasp`); [Microsoft Learn — Minimal APIs](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/aspnet/core/fundamentals/minimal-apis) (`minimal`); [Microsoft Entra identity platform](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity-platform/) (`identity`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Authorization stays in ASP.NET — C#:**
```csharp
app.MapGet("/api/orders/{id:int}", async (
    int id, ClaimsPrincipal currentUser, IOrderService orders) =>
{
    var order = await orders.GetForUserAsync(id, currentUser);
    return order is null ? Results.NotFound() : Results.Ok(order);
}).RequireAuthorization();
// Add using System.Security.Claims; your service enforces ownership.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

- **Understand:** IDs and strings from the model are untrusted.

#### Module 39: Tool chains and controlled workflows — Days 191–195 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build a two-step tool workflow
- **Why it matters:** One user request may need several reads, but your app owns the boundaries.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** A service orchestrator can call GetCustomer then GetOrders then GetShipment.
- **Example:** “Show my latest order and ETA” needs multiple business lookups.
- **What to build:** Build a two-step tool workflow
- **Concepts introduced:** Tool orchestration, Multi-step tools, Bounded loop, Timeout, Workflow
- **Supports practical project:** `p6` — AI Order Tool Assistant
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Learn — Agent Framework course](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/) (`agentstart`); [Agent Framework — .NET workflows](https://github.com/microsoft/agent-framework/tree/main/dotnet/samples/03-workflows) (`agentworkflow`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Explicit C# tool chain with a maximum step count:**
```csharp
const int maxToolSteps = 3;
int steps = 0;
foreach (var requestedTool in new[] { "GetOrder", "GetShipment" })
{
    if (++steps > maxToolSteps) throw new InvalidOperationException();
    Console.WriteLine($"Validate and authorize {requestedTool}");
}
// Execute only tools your backend explicitly allows.
```

**Day 191: One call or several?** — *Understand*

- **Definition of done / proof:** Sequential tool method
- **Read next:** [OpenAI official .NET SDK](https://github.com/openai/openai-dotnet) (`openai`); [Microsoft Learn — Agent Framework course](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/agent-framework/get-started/) (`agentstart`); [Agent Framework — .NET workflows](https://github.com/microsoft/agent-framework/tree/main/dotnet/samples/03-workflows) (`agentworkflow`)

#### Module 40: Turn text into embeddings — Days 196–200 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build a simple embedding generator app

- **Example:** “Headphones” and “audio listening device” should yield related embeddings.
- **What to build:** Build a simple embedding generator app
- **Concepts introduced:** Embedding, Embedding model, Dimension, Semantic similarity, Model version
- **Supports practical project:** `p6` — AI Order Tool Assistant
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.


#### Module 41: Semantic product search in memory — Days 201–205 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build Semantic Product Search
- **Why it matters:** Semantic search returns related descriptions even without exact keyword matches.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** LINQ sorting by cosine similarity is enough to learn the basic idea.
- **Example:** “Device for online meetings” should surface webcams and microphones.
- **What to build:** Build Semantic Product Search
- **Concepts introduced:** Semantic search, Cosine ranking, Top-k, Embedding cache, Keyword baseline
- **Supports practical project:** `p7` — Semantic Product Search
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Rank products with C# LINQ:**
```csharp
var topFive = products
    .Select(p => new { Product = p,
        Score = Cosine(queryVector, p.Embedding) })
    .OrderByDescending(x => x.Score)
    .Take(5)
    .ToList();
// Product/Embedding/Cosine are types you created in earlier labs.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 201: Create product inventory** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** You need text descriptions that represent each product.

**Day 203: Embed a search query** — *Write C# / build*

### PHASE 08: Search fundamentals and your first RAG app — Days 211–240
#### Module 43: Keyword search, BM25 and why vectors are not enough — Days 211–215 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Add a keyword-search baseline
- **Why it matters:** Exact terms, identifiers and recent names may work better with keyword search.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** SQL LIKE / full-text index answers different questions than semantic search.
- **Example:** Search “INV-2042” exactly; semantic matching may rank the wrong invoice.
- **What to build:** Add a keyword-search baseline
- **Concepts introduced:** Keyword search, Inverted index, BM25, Filters, Exact match
- **Supports practical project:** `p8` — Vector Search API
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — Azure AI Search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/search-what-is-azure-search) (`azsearch`); [Microsoft Learn — Hybrid search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/hybrid-search-overview) (`azhybrid`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Start with keyword retrieval in pure LINQ:**
```csharp
string query = "refund";
string[] documents = ["Our refund policy", "Shipping times"];
var matches = documents.Where(x =>
    x.Contains(query, StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase));
// BM25 is a more sophisticated term-ranking algorithm.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 211: Exact term vs meaning** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Full text search finds literal words, while embeddings capture broader meaning.
- **Do:** Test “invoice INV-2042” against keyword and semantic search.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Two result lists
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Azure AI Search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/search-what-is-azure-search) (`azsearch`); [Microsoft Learn — Hybrid search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/hybrid-search-overview) (`azhybrid`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Day 212: Inverted index concept** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Keyword engines map terms to documents for fast lookups.
- **Do:** Make a tiny Dictionary<string,List<int>> inverted index in C#.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Toy inverted index
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Azure AI Search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/search-what-is-azure-search) (`azsearch`); [Microsoft Learn — Hybrid search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/hybrid-search-overview) (`azhybrid`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Day 213: BM25 is a ranking method** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** BM25 rewards matched terms with useful weighting, not simple substring presence.
- **Do:** Read Microsoft Azure hybrid overview; summarize BM25 in one sentence.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Plain-English BM25 note
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Azure AI Search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/search-what-is-azure-search) (`azsearch`); [Microsoft Learn — Hybrid search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/hybrid-search-overview) (`azhybrid`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Day 214: Filters and scoring** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** Date, category, tenant and exact status constrain search.

#### Module 44: Hybrid search, RRF and reranking — Days 216–220 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build a small hybrid search experiment
- **Why it matters:** Hybrid merges keyword + vector candidates; a reranker orders the final shortlist.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Two different search rankings become one final ordered list.
- **Example:** For “late invoice policy”, match both invoice ID and related policy semantics.
- **What to build:** Build a small hybrid search experiment
- **Concepts introduced:** Hybrid search, RRF, Reranker, Semantic ranker, Recall
- **Supports practical project:** `p8` — Vector Search API
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — Hybrid search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/hybrid-search-overview) (`azhybrid`); [Microsoft Learn — Azure AI Search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/search-what-is-azure-search) (`azsearch`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Combine two result lists before asking the LLM:**
```csharp
string[] keywordMatches = ["doc1", "doc3"];
string[] vectorMatches = ["doc2", "doc1"];
var candidates = keywordMatches.Concat(vectorMatches).Distinct();
// In production, use RRF/reranking rather than simply Distinct().
```

#### Module 46: Chunk documents without losing meaning — Days 226–230 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Test three chunking approaches
- **Why it matters:** Documents must be split into searchable pieces, but boundaries can break answers.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** It is like paginating a large log: splitting in the wrong place hides related events.
- **Example:** Keep a refund limit and its exception in the same chunk if possible.
- **What to build:** Test three chunking approaches
- **Concepts introduced:** Chunking, Chunk size, Chunk overlap, Section metadata, Parent-child
- **Supports practical project:** `p8` — Vector Search API
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Simple character chunking — learning only:**
```csharp
static IEnumerable<string> Chunks(string text, int length)
{
    for (int i = 0; i < text.Length; i += length)
        yield return text.Substring(i, Math.Min(length, text.Length - i));
}
// Production RAG should split by tokens/meaning and track page metadata.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 226: Why chunk?** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Large documents may exceed useful retrieval/context size.
- **Do:** Split an article into chunks of 300 characters; count pieces.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Fixed-size chunks
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Day 227: Token-based and overlap** — *Try a tiny example*
- **Understand:** Tokens are closer to model context limits than characters.
- **Do:** Compare chunks with zero overlap and with 50-character overlap.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Overlap comparison
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Day 228: Respect headings and sections** — *Write C# / build*
- **Understand:** Sentence/heading splits preserve local meaning better.
- **Do:** Split a Markdown policy by heading and attach heading metadata.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Section-aware chunks
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Day 229: Parent/child strategy** — *Test your understanding*
- **Understand:** Smaller search snippets can link back to larger context.
- **Do:** Store ParentSectionId and ChildChunkId in C# records.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Parent/child DTOs
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Day 230: Measure chunk impact** — *Finish and explain*
- **Understand:** “Best” chunk size depends on documents and questions.
- **Do:** Test three chunk sizes against 6 policy questions and record retrieval quality.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Chunk experiment table
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

#### Module 47: Understand basic RAG as two separate pipelines — Days 231–235 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build your Basic PDF Q&A assistant
- **Why it matters:** RAG first retrieves relevant context, then asks an LLM to answer from that context.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Search is the data layer; the LLM is an answer composer.
- **Example:** User asks leave policy → retrieve policy text → model summarizes with source.
- **What to build:** Build your Basic PDF Q&A assistant
- **Concepts introduced:** RAG, Ingestion pipeline, Retrieval pipeline, Grounding, Top-k
- **Supports practical project:** `p8` — Vector Search API
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Two separate RAG flows:**
```csharp
INGEST (once per document):
File -> Extract -> Clean -> Chunk -> Embed -> Index

QUERY (once per user question):
Question -> Embed -> Search authorized chunks
         -> Context + citations -> LLM -> Answer

If evidence is missing: abstain.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 231: Ingestion time vs query time** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** File processing should not be repeated for every question.
- **Do:** Draw separate INGEST and QUERY flowcharts.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Two pipeline diagrams
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`); [Generative AI for Beginners — .NET](https://github.com/microsoft/Generative-AI-for-beginners-dotnet) (`genai`)

#### Module 48: Citations, no-answer behavior and document lifecycle — Days 236–240 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Make RAG verifiable and updateable
- **Why it matters:** A confident answer without evidence is a weak enterprise AI experience.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Citations should behave like foreign keys into actual stored source records.
- **Example:** “Employees have 20 days leave [HR Policy p.3]” can be checked.
- **What to build:** Make RAG verifiable and updateable
- **Concepts introduced:** Citation, Provenance, Abstention, Document version, Index deletion
- **Supports practical project:** `p9` — Basic Document RAG Assistant
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`); [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications](https://genai.owasp.org/llm-top-10/) (`owasp`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Citation is data, not decorative text:**
```csharp
public sealed record Evidence(string DocumentId, int Page, string Quote);
public sealed record RagResponse(string Answer, Evidence[] Sources);
// Show original source to user. Never fabricate a page number.
```

### PHASE 09: Make search and RAG production-ready — Days 241–270
#### Module 49: Measure retrieval before tuning prompts — Days 241–245 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Create a retrieval evaluation harness
- **Why it matters:** Bad evidence in means bad answers out. Evaluate document retrieval on its own.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Test search like a ranking feature: expected document IDs are your assertions.
- **Example:** Question “How long for refunds?” should retrieve ReturnsPolicy.pdf.
- **What to build:** Create a retrieval evaluation harness
- **Concepts introduced:** Golden dataset, Recall@k, Precision@k, MRR, nDCG
- **Supports practical project:** `p10` — RAG Quality Test Harness
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Microsoft Learn — Hybrid search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/hybrid-search-overview) (`azhybrid`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Recall@K — C#:**
```csharp
static double RecallAtK(
    IEnumerable<string> returned,
    HashSet<string> relevant, int k)
{
    if (relevant.Count == 0) return 1;
    int found = returned.Take(k).Count(relevant.Contains);
    return (double)found / relevant.Count;
}
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 241: Make a retrieval golden dataset** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** For each question, record relevant source IDs and passages.
- **Do:** Create 30 questions mapped to relevant chunk or document IDs.
- **Definition of done / proof:** 30 labeled retrieval cases
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Microsoft Learn — Hybrid search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/hybrid-search-overview) (`azhybrid`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

**Day 242: Recall@k and Precision@k** — *Try a tiny example*

- **Do:** Write C# RecallAtK and PrecisionAtK for one query.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Two tested metrics
- **Read next:** [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`); [Microsoft Learn — Hybrid search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/hybrid-search-overview) (`azhybrid`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`)

#### Module 50: Better RAG queries, reranking and complex questions — Days 246–250 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Improve retrieval with query rewriting and reranking
- **Why it matters:** A user question may need rewriting, decomposition or multiple pieces of evidence.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Like a repository search: sometimes split a complex requirement into smaller queries.
- **Example:** “Which warranty applies to items purchased last quarter?” combines policy + date.
- **What to build:** Improve retrieval with query rewriting and reranking
- **Concepts introduced:** Query rewriting, Multi-query, Question decomposition, Reranking, Context precision
- **Supports practical project:** `p10` — RAG Quality Test Harness
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.

```csharp
}

```

#### Module 51: Tenant isolation, search access and secure RAG — Days 251–255 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Build enterprise document access filters
- **Why it matters:** Retrieval security must be enforced before snippets reach the LLM.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** TenantId is not just a UI filter: it must be part of the server-side query and authorization.
- **Example:** Customer A may never retrieve Customer B’s invoice, even via prompt injection.
- **What to build:** Build enterprise document access filters
- **Concepts introduced:** Tenant isolation, ACL, Identity filter, Authorization, Data exfiltration
- **Supports practical project:** `p11` — Advanced Enterprise RAG
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft Learn — Azure AI Search](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/search-what-is-azure-search) (`azsearch`); [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`); [OWASP Top 10 for LLM Applications](https://genai.owasp.org/llm-top-10/) (`owasp`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Filter by identity before vector retrieval:**
```csharp
public sealed record SearchRequest(string Text, string TenantId);
// TenantId must come from verified server-side identity.
// Never trust a TenantId supplied by the user's prompt.
```

#### Module 52: Keep RAG documents fresh and indexes maintainable — Days 256–260 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Implement background document reindexing
- **Why it matters:** Indexing is a data pipeline with versions, updates, retries and failures.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** Treat ingestion as a background ETL job with an idempotent database write.
- **Example:** Updating an HR policy should replace stale vector chunks, not append duplicates.
- **What to build:** Implement background document reindexing
- **Concepts introduced:** Data pipeline, BackgroundService, Idempotency, Embedding version, Retry queue
- **Supports practical project:** `p11` — Advanced Enterprise RAG
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`); [Microsoft Learn — Azure Blob Storage for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/blobs/storage-quickstart-blobs-dotnet) (`azureblob`); [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Versioning is essential for fresh knowledge:**
```csharp
public record IndexedDocument(
    string DocumentId, int Version, string ContentHash, DateTimeOffset IndexedAt);
// Updating or deleting a file must update/delete all related chunks.
```
**Day-by-day tasks, explanations and proof:**

**Day 256: Track documents and versions** — *Understand*
- **Understand:** Use source hash, version, updated time and status.
- **Do:** Create DocumentRecord with SHA256 and IndexStatus fields.
- **Definition of done / proof:** Document manifest schema
- **Read next:** [Azure RAG demo — ASP.NET + Blazor (C#)](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/azure-search-openai-demo-csharp) (`ragcsharp`); [Microsoft Learn — Azure Blob Storage for .NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/storage/blobs/storage-quickstart-blobs-dotnet) (`azureblob`); [Microsoft Learn — Retrieval-augmented generation](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/search/retrieval-augmented-generation-overview) (`azrag`)

#### Module 54: Refactor with Microsoft.Extensions.AI and .NET patterns — Days 266–270 (Intermediate)
- **Goal / deliverable:** Refactor RAG/chat into provider-neutral .NET services
- **Why it matters:** Once raw model calls are clear, interfaces help swap providers and add middleware.
- **Simple .NET comparison:** IChatClient is like HttpClient abstraction; DI controls lifetimes and test substitutes.
- **Example:** The same Blazor app can use a fake or real chat provider behind IChatClient.
- **What to build:** Refactor RAG/chat into provider-neutral .NET services
- **Concepts introduced:** IChatClient, IEmbeddingGenerator, Dependency injection, Adapter, Middleware
- **Supports practical project:** `p11` — Advanced Enterprise RAG
- **Common mistake to avoid:** Trying to memorize terms instead of running a tiny .NET experiment.
- **Official / external references:** [Microsoft.Extensions.AI — Microsoft Learn](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/ai/microsoft-extensions-ai) (`mea`); [Microsoft.Extensions.AI — source](https://github.com/dotnet/extensions/tree/main/src/Libraries/Microsoft.Extensions.AI.Abstractions) (`measrc`); [.NET AI samples](https://github.com/dotnet/ai-samples) (`aisamples`)

**Existing C# starter illustration — Provider independence — C# architecture:**
```csharp
// Microsoft.Extensions.AI provides abstractions:
// IChatClient and IEmbeddingGenerator<TInput,TEmbedding>
// Use DI to inject chat, embeddings and instrumentation.
// Keep your own domain/business logic independent of provider.
```
